#7329. Sales Engineer, ANZ at Coalesce

Collaborating closely with the sales team, you will be the bridge between our technology and our clients, delivering compelling technical demonstrations and guiding customers towards successful implementations. Your ability to showcase the value and capabilities of Coalesce's offerings will be instrumental in driving growth and customer satisfaction. You will primarily cover Australia and New Zealand, working alongside Account Executives and the Partner team.

Engage in technical discussions with prospective clients to understand their unique challenges and data requirements.

Own technical relationship with prospects throughout the sales process. Run discovery, demos, and POCs. Lead architecture reviews.

Develop and deliver compelling technical presentations, showcasing the capabilities and features of Coalesce's solutions.

Collaborate with customers to understand their data ecosystem, workflows, and integration points, providing tailored solutions to address their needs.

Craft and present technical proposals that effectively communicate the benefits of Coalesce's technology in solving complex business problems.

Serve as the technical liaison between clients and internal teams, conveying customer feedback and contributing to product enhancement discussions.

Stay up-to-date with industry trends, competitive landscape, and emerging technologies such as AI to effectively position Coalesce's offerings.

Continuously build expertise in Coalesce's technical features, use cases, and integration possibilities.

5+ years in data engineering, analytics engineering or data platform roles, including at least 2 years as a Sales Engineer, Solutions Engineer or similar technical sales role.

Strong technical understanding of data engineering concepts, ETL processes, and data transformation methodologies.

Experience with MCP and governing AI agents' in building and managing data infrastructure. Strong technical understanding of agentic AI and how it is used in data engineering.

Familiarity with data governance and data observability concepts and their relationship with data engineering

Proficiency in SQL and demonstrated experience working with Snowflake and the Snowflake partner ecosystem, or other cloud data platform such as Databricks or Google BigQuery.

Excellent communication skills, with the ability to convey complex technical concepts to both technical and non-technical audiences.

Demonstrated problem-solving skills and a consultative approach to understanding and addressing customer challenges.

Self-motivated, proactive, and results-driven mindset with a focus on driving customer success.

Willingness to travel as needed to support customer engagements and attend industry events.

Experience with version control systems (e.g., Git) and collaborative development practices.

Bachelor's degree in a technical field, such as Computer Science, Engineering, or related disciplines (or equivalent practical experience).

Competitive salary and performance-based incentives.

Contribution toward private health insurance.

Employer superannuation contributions in line with the Superannuation Guarantee.

Flexible work arrangements and remote work options.

Professional development opportunities and continuous learning.

Collaborative and innovative work environment.

#7325. Brands race to systematize how to know what makes creator content worth paying for

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Social media trends move faster than brands can keep up with. Now, they’re trying to create repeatable systems to turn organic social attention into something they can evaluate, fund and ultimately scale.

Take General Mills, for example. Home to brands like Cheerios, Cinnamon Toast Crunch and Trix, the CPG brand has spent the past year putting together an internal framework to systematize monetizing viral moments.

Its “talkability score” is a calculation used to measure whether General Mills’ brands are earning more attention with social media creators than they’re buying with paid ads. It’s a loose framework that varies by brand, Mindy Murray, director of influencer and content at General Mills said, and generates a score based on whether users shared it, put their own spin on it and how long interest in the trend lasts.

Murray was mum on the actual mechanics on how it actually determines whether to invest ad spend. “We’re weighing on earning, we’re weighing things like active engagement — so shares likes, comments, saves — because we know that that means someone took an actual effort to do something versus just an impression,” Murray said.

Virality can’t be manufactured, but this “operating system” allows them to “go faster and be smarter,” said Murray.

Over the past year, General Mills has also reorganized its teams to bring social and influencer marketing under Murray’s purview, she told Digiday, to move more quickly and efficiently in converting buzzy organic content into paid ads.

For example, over the summer Reese’s “Eat ‘Em Up” cereal jingle from the early 2000s started trending on TikTok. General Mills noticed the trend, partnered with GloRilla to re-record the rap, tracked real-time activity via its framework, and ultimately turned it into a campaign on streaming platforms and Reese’s Puffs’ YouTube channel. The company boasted a 2.5% year-over-year sales increase as a result, but did not provide specific sales figures. The company did not say specifically how the framework informed that strategy.

Last week, influencer agency Billion Dollar Boy (BDB) rolled out its Creator Investment Planner, a financial modeling tool that aims to help marketers understand the ad spend levels required to plan and “de-risk creator investments,” according to a news release.

BDB’s tool works by calculating blended CPMs from creators and social media platforms. Marketers enter their budget and time frame, and the platform calculates estimates for share of voice, total impressions and earned media value. Per BDB, the model can update as inputs change, where marketers can expect to “de-risk” spend commitments.

And in April, CreatorIQ creator platform and creative analytics and governance platform CreativeX launched a new tool to help brands incorporate creator content into a paid media strategy. The system works on an API, sending creator content from CreatorIQ’s platform to CreativeX to be graded, reviewed by CreativeX’s AI-powered system and optimized by a brand campaign manager. Ultimately, it aims to identify creator posts with potential to do well as an ad before any media buyers open their wallets. Nestlé was the first brand to sign on. The integration was announced on an invite-only basis.

To agency execs, all signs point to brands getting more rigorous about their spend — especially when it comes to squeezing performance out of creators — and do it before the trend disappears.

Creator spend is expected to increase 12% every year until 2030, according to advertising research firm Madison & Wall. In no small part, brand spend on boosting or promoting creator content is fueling that growth.

“Before any sort of budget is unlocked, you have to be able to prove out that there’s a higher propensity for that piece of content for whatever investment you’re making into social to actually move towards a conversion,” said Madison Gaudry-Routledge, evp of social at Viral Nation, a social agency.

Most brands, Gaudry-Routledge said, haven’t come up with a named framework in the same way General Mills has. Brand spend on creator marketing is expected to grow twice as fast as spending on advertising. Naturally, brands are eager to see proof of concept as early as possible.

Viral Nation has its own proprietary framework offered to clients as an add-on service. It’s called Culture Quotient that blends brand awareness, brand affinity and brand velocity into a measurable score to determine a brand’s relevance in cultural conversation. The tool works via AI that analyzes conversations and content, identifies emerging cultural trends and tracks shifts in consumer sentiment, per Gaudry-Routledge. The social exec declined to share any client-specific examples.

These calculations are being built in real time and haven’t yet replaced traditional metrics like share of voice, audience sentiment and engagement.

“As an industry, we’re continuing to try to catch up on operationalizing frameworks and systematizing measurement and still not there yet,” said Kristina Coughlin, svp and general manager of Trevant, a creator marketing agency.

Dick’s is betting that youth sports can fuel its next phase of ad growth through GameChanger—if it can overcome limits on scale.

As Black Friday Cyber Monday rapidly approaches, brands are most focused on acquiring new customers this holiday season.

Product discovery habits are changing, leaving marketers unsure how to pivot ahead of the biggest sales period of the year.

We're hiring a Nurse Practitioner to serve as the primary clinical point of contact for patients navigating their remote care journey.

In this role, you will lead clinical decision-making with a strong emphasis on guideline-directed medical therapy for heart failure, as well as adherence to evidence-based guidelines for type 2 diabetes and hypertension, including proactive and asynchronous medication titration based on patient vitals, symptoms, and trends. #7321. The gaming creator with more than 26 million followers just launched his own holding company

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This article is part of an ongoing series examining successful creator business strategies. More from the series →

Cody “Clix” Conrod is a professional Fortnite player who regularly wins esports tournaments, and is also a wildly popular creator. Conrod started playing video games at five years old, and began live streaming at 13. Conrod has amassed some 26 million followers across social media since (8.73 million followers on Twitch, over 5 million on YouTube, 6.7 million on TikTok, 2.86 million on X, 2.81 million on Instagram, and 848,000 on Snapchat).

He’s the most-watched professional Fortnite player in the world, according to his team, but in the last few years, he’s been looking to grow beyond just esports content and branded partnerships with a portfolio of his own companies.

While still with talent agency Vayner Sports, Conrod was paired with agent Darren Glover, who said he was influenced by the career movements made by creators like Jimmy “MrBeast” Donaldson and the Dude Perfect team (who were forming their own entities and corporations). Glover wanted to apply this same approach to Conrod’s career — prioritizing fewer, more quality partnerships.

“I tried to actually do half and half. I tried to be a halftime Fortnite professional and then a halftime content creator that also wants to work with brands. And I became not good at the game,” Conrod said, which pushed him to retain Glover.

Glover was named CEO of Full Box Inc., the Conrod-led holding company that launched in early October, with Conrod as chief creative officer. Heading into this fall, Conrod was running his entire creator business through Clix Gaming, his creator-focused gaming media company. Full Box Inc. combines Conrod’s nine creator-focused companies and organizes them under three pillars: operating companies (Clix Gaming, Clix Creative, and Dr3amin Clothing), Full Box Ventures (Reach Clipping, Real Sports App, water filtration company Rorra, TapCaps PC collectibles, and lifestyle brand XSET), and Impact (Dr3amin Access Fund).

“The change was not starting something new, it was adding an umbrella over [the] top of everything,” said Glover.

Conrod may be following in the footsteps of other massive creators with millions of followers, but his positioning as a professional gamer first, content creator second, means his approach has to consider both professions. Sometimes, Conrod can’t stream his Fortnite gameplay during tournaments, as creators playing online games can often be “stream sniped,” which is when viewers watch a broadcast while also playing the same game and use the stream to find and kill the streamer in-game. This can cause players who are stream-sniped to lose tournaments. Glover helped Conrod scale his content to a point that, if he does go to a Fortnite tournament and isn’t streaming, he has a backlog of things to post that doesn’t require him to work late into the night to produce something post-tournament.

The holdco is the biggest step of many Conrod has taken to ensure his career can extend far beyond professional gaming. It’s one many video game creators may take in the future as they seek to generate revenue that isn’t tied to game updates or platform algorithms — or even their own face. Consider it the MrBeast business model.

“I realized when I was 18, I wanted to build stuff larger than just me, where it could scale without my face all the time or me having to be there,” said Conrod.

Conrod and Glover don’t just want to create products and agencies and sign brand deals, but extend into the philanthropy side of the creator space to cement his legacy.

Conrod launched the Dr3amin Access Fund, which provides kids with the access and hardware needed to become professional gamers or gaming streamers. Last year, the fund delivered more than $250,000 in gaming equipment.

For now, partnerships still make up around half of Conrod’s revenue, with the balance split between ad revenue on Twitch, YouTube, owned royalties, and Fortnite’s unique Support-A-Creator program, which lets players back creators in Fortnite by giving them 5 percent of purchases made with their unique creator code. They did not share revenue specifics.

“Our business is profitable and runs without one single brand dollar,” said Glover. “So brand dollars for us are investment vehicles of partnerships for us to do more, but we’re not putting ourselves at risk of being profitable or scaling our company based on other people’s dollars.”

He wants more creators to understand that they have some control over their revenue on platforms like YouTube and Twitch, or, in Conrod’s case, Fortnite, and that they can expand beyond just a basic sponsorship setup.

“I want content people to feel like they are a major factor for the health of the business,” Glover added, “and I think we’ve been showing that we’re doing that, and we’re doing it really well.”

A look at how GTA 6 has impacted the creator economy, with stream numbers, viewership, top creators, and organic hype.

The creator economy is shifting its stance on clipping as platforms crackdown; what does clipping look like in the future?

#7319. Software Engineer - AI Tooling Infrastructure at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re looking for a Software Engineer, AI Tooling Infrastructure to join our AI Tooling team and help build and run the backend services behind Supabase’s AI products. The Supabase MCP server is one example of what the team runs in production, alongside the services that support agent skills and the dashboard assistant. You’ll work closely with the Platform, SRE, Auth, Billing and Edge Functions teams, and with the engineers on the AI Tooling team who build the product surfaces.

The AI Tooling team runs its own services in production and carries its own on-call. Traffic from AI clients and agents behaves differently from traffic from people: hundreds of calls a minute, retry loops, and no reading of deprecation warnings. This role makes sure the services they use are reliable, correctly authorized and observable. It is a systems and infrastructure role rather than a product AI role, and it is not an SRE role. You will build and operate these systems, not only advise on them.

This role is ideal for someone who thrives in async, fast-paced environments, owns what they ship in production, and is excited about building developer tools that scale to millions.

Build and operate the production backend services behind Supabase’s AI products, including the MCP server: deployment, scaling, configuration and release.

Take ownership of how those services are structured, including splitting or reshaping a service when that makes it safer to run and easier to change.

Define, measure and meet reliability targets for the services the team runs. Set SLOs with the SRE team, track error budgets, and use them to decide what to fix first.

Participate in the AI Tooling team on-call rotation, and be a first responder for incidents in those services. Investigate, fix, coordinate with other teams during incidents, and write the follow-ups and runbooks.

Work the AI Tooling team support queue alongside on-call, and turn recurring issues into fixes rather than repeated manual answers.

Own authentication and authorization for AI and agent clients: OAuth apps, scoped tokens, and permissions that make sense for agents as well as humans, including how we change or deprecate a permission without breaking existing integrations.

Own observability for the services the team runs: metrics, traces, logs, dashboards and alerts, so we find problems before customers do.

Handle capacity and abuse for agent traffic: rate limits, cost and safety controls, and behavior under load or when a dependency degrades.

Improve the architecture of the services the team owns as they grow, including splitting or reshaping a service when that makes it safer to run and easier to change.

Work with the Platform team on shared code, review ownership and release process, and with the SRE team on infrastructure, alerting and incident practice.

Contribute to the code of the AI Tooling team’s tools, such as the MCP server, where it affects reliability and security, in partnership with the engineers who own the product surface.

Have 5+ years of experience building and running backend services in production, and have carried on-call for them.

Write strong TypeScript or Go, and are comfortable working in the other.

Have designed and operated APIs with real consumers: versioning, error handling, rate limiting, and backwards-compatible change.

Know OAuth, OIDC, JWT and API authorization well enough to debug them in production and to explain where implementations commonly deviate from the spec.

Have a good understanding of PostgreSQL and relational databases.

Are comfortable with CI/CD, Docker and containerized deployments, and with infrastructure as code. Any tool is fine.

Can debug across services: follow a slow or failing request through several systems using metrics, traces and logs.

Communicate clearly in writing, work with a wide range of stakeholders, and thrive in async, globally distributed teams.

Are comfortable navigating ambiguity and moving quickly.

Pick your centre of gravity. We expect you to be deep in at least one of these, and to tell us which:

APIs and auth: You have designed permission models, token scopes or OAuth flows for third-party or machine clients, and you have migrated them without breaking users.

Operations: You have run production services yourself: carried the pager, written alerts and runbooks, used SLOs or similar to decide what to fix, and made shared systems less risky over time.

Gateways and distributed systems: You have built or run proxies or gateways, or debugged hard failures across services (retries, partial failures, upstream outages, fail-open vs fail-closed decisions), and you can reason about load and degradation.

Experience with agent tooling (MCP or similar), or serving traffic from AI agents and LLM clients.

Experience moving a service out of a larger codebase while keeping existing clients working.

Experience with Grafana, OpenTelemetry, Logflare or a similar observability stack, and real SLO work.

Open-source contributions, especially to Node.js, Go, TypeScript or API tooling.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Deep lifecycle marketing mastery: significant experience building and optimizing automated email and SMS flows (onboarding, retention, win-backs), rather than just writing one-off promotional campaigns. You know how to nurture a lead over time.

A strong foundation in A/B testing mechanics — comfortable formulating clear copy hypotheses, understanding sample size requirements, and knowing when a test result has achieved true statistical significance.

The balance of empathy and compliance: a proven ability to write about highly sensitive personal health topics (menopause symptoms, hormonal changes, wellness) with profound empathy while strictly adhering to healthcare compliance rules and ad-network medical restrictions.

An experimenter’s ego — you don’t take copy critiques personally. You’re obsessed with letting consumer behavior and performance data crown the winning angle.

#7269. Senior Forward Deployed Engineer at Clutch

Clutch builds AI agents that work on behalf of credit unions. Emma, our collections agent, is live at five credit unions and handled 60,000+ member conversations in her first twelve weeks at one of them. Behind her is a growing portfolio: origination, fraud step-up, member (re)engagement and activation agents, all built on the same Agents Platform (TypeScript, NestJS, our own agent SDK, MCP, Temporal, promptfoo).

As a FDE you are one of the two engineers on an agent. You take it from "sold" to "live and performing" at a specific credit union: you sit in the discovery sessions, you build the integrations to their core banking and loan systems, you author and tune the agent's behavior, you run the live tests with their staff, and you stay with it through go-live and the first weeks of production. When something needs a person in the room, you are the one who flies.

This is a senior engineering role, not a solutions or support role. You write production code in our monorepo every week: integrations, modules, and the agents themselves (prompts, tools, evals), together with the other engineers on the pod. The difference from a traditional engineer is that you are on site more often, you make judgment calls in front of the customer, and you own the outcome. What you learn in the field flows straight back into the product: defaults, playbooks, and the platform capabilities the next agent will need.

You build; our AI Operations Managers deliver. The pod (pod lead plus engineers) designs and builds each agent in a high-touch way with the first customers. Once the agent is defined and live, AI Ops coordinates the implementations, configuration and ongoing success at the next credit unions. You hand them something that works and a playbook to run it.

This role requires roughly 20% of travel on average, with one- to two-week stretches around kickoffs and go-lives. Based in the US, able to travel on short notice

You'll join the Agents team, a small, senior group operating as a startup inside Clutch: seven engineers, a dedicated AI product group, and AI sales specialists, working directly with some of the largest credit unions in the country. Every agent is staffed by a pair of engineers, one leaning toward the conversation and LLM side, one toward software and integrations, with a pod lead who owns the product day to day. There is no layer between you and the customer, and no layer between you and the platform team.

The operating style is direct, written, and evidence-driven: BLUF updates, evals over opinions, business outcomes over ticket counts. The team is spread across Brazil, Argentina and the US and works async by default. Being on site is a tool we use at specific moments (kickoff, live testing, go-live), not a permanent posting.

Ship your first production change to a live agent through the team's review process, and be able to explain how a member's call flows through the Agents Platform end to end

Learn one agent deeply: system prompt, tool contracts, orchestration triggers, compliance gates, and the implementation process from kickoff to go-live

Join at least one credit union implementation as the second engineer, including a live testing session with their staff

Map where the current implementation process loses time and where a default or a tool would have removed a round trip

Own the technical side of one credit union implementation end to end: core and LOS integration, agent configuration, testing, go-live, and the first weeks of production

Run the eval and tuning loop for your agent (golden transcripts, rubric judges, regression suite) and be trusted to change its behavior without a second engineer checking your work

Have completed at least two on-site visits and turned what you heard there into product decisions with your pod lead and PM

Contribute at least one reusable capability back to the Agents Platform that the next implementation will not have to rebuild

Have taken one net-new agent from sold to live at its first credit union, with data you can show the partner

Be the engineer credit union staff ask for by name when something in their deployment needs to change

Have written the implementation playbook for your agent well enough that the AI Ops team runs the second and third credit union without you

Be shaping what the next agents look like, because you have seen where the current ones break in the field

7+ years of software engineering in a fast-paced environment, preferably in SaaS and/or Fintech, with senior TypeScript and Node.js backend experience in a large codebase (NestJS, CQRS, schema-validated boundaries, dependency injection). We do not count years of AI experience; the field is too young for that to mean anything. We look at what you have shipped and how you reason about it

Integration and API engineering: secure service-to-service endpoints, third-party APIs, webhooks, and the patience to work with legacy banking systems that were not designed to be integrated

Security and auth judgment: OAuth and machine-to-machine auth, tenant-scoped access, secrets handling, no PII in logs. Our agents touch sensitive member data and operate under consumer-contact regulation

Customer presence: you can sit across from a VP of Collections, hear a request, and say "here is what I recommend and why", including when the answer is no. You are comfortable making calls in the room and owning them afterwards

Code-quality judgment under deadline pressure: you take on debt deliberately, name it, and pay it down, rather than gold-plating or leaving silent debt behind

Genuine appetite for LLM agents: eval discipline for non-deterministic systems, reading a system prompt like code, understanding that some decisions must never reach the model. Curiosity plus shipped work is enough; research depth is not required

Nice to have, explicitly not required: voice and telephony (LiveKit, Twilio), Temporal or event-driven orchestration, Python, core banking or LOS integration experience, credit union or banking domain knowledge

You'll work directly with senior leaders to shape the GTM systems roadmap, turn business strategy into technical plans, and guide decisions that span multiple teams. You'll also help grow the engineers around you, setting the technical standards and practices the team builds on.

You understand the difference between owning a system and gatekeeping one. Sales lives in Salesforce. Marketing lives in HubSpot. Your job is to make sure both are governed well enough that others can work in them effectively, and powerful enough that the GTM team can do things they couldn't do before.

You'll thrive as a Staff GTM Systems Engineer if you:

Are known as the go-to person for tough technical problems, and have good judgment about which ones matter most.

Think in systems and long time horizons, and can make confident architectural decisions with incomplete information.

Can clearly explain complex technical concepts and tradeoffs to engineers and executives alike.

Believe systems should be managed with engineering discipline.

Ship fast and reliably — your work holds up in production.

Are a fast learner, frequently picking up new languages and tools.

Find real satisfaction in mentoring other engineers and helping them grow.

Own the architecture of the GTM systems stack: define how Salesforce, HubSpot, enrichment, sales intelligence, billing, and the data platform fit together, and write the technical strategy that guides how it evolves.

Lead build-vs-buy and procurement decisions: evaluate vendors, run proofs of concept, assess security and integration fit, and partner with Director of GTM Systems and Analytics, Finance, Legal, and IT on selection and renewals.

Shape the GTM systems roadmap with senior leaders across Sales, Marketing, RevOps, Finance, and Engineering: align on requirements, sequence investments, make clear tradeoffs, and lead cross-functional initiatives to completion.

Own Salesforce as the engineering system of record for revenue: data model, flows and automation, validation rules, field governance, and access control.

Own HubSpot for campaigns and lead generation, along with the HubSpot-Salesforce sync — so leads, contacts, and engagement signals reach the revenue pipeline without drift or silent failures.

Build and maintain integrations across the GTM stack, and partner with GTM Analytics Engineering on reliable data flows to the central data platform.

Design and deploy agentic workflows that consume GTM data, surface actionable signals, and automate manual operations — and set the patterns and guardrails others build on.

Establish observability and change management for critical GTM systems: alerts, dashboards, and runbooks; reviewed code changes, documented config, and rollback paths.

Mentor engineers across experience levels and set the technical standards for the team.

Partner with the Billing Systems Engineer to automate the CPQ-to-Billing handoff.

Extensive engineering experience in GTM or business systems, including owning a GTM stack's architecture end to end — build-vs-buy calls, vendor evaluations, and systems designed for a multi-year horizon.

Deep, hands-on Salesforce expertise — you understand the data model, have built complex flows and automation, and you think about it as an engineering system, not an admin tool. This is the primary bar.

HubSpot experience in a marketing context, including the HubSpot-Salesforce sync and what breaks and why.

Technical depth beyond the UI — you've built integrations with APIs, webhooks, and event streams, and you know when a native tool (a Flow, a Clay table, a HubSpot workflow) is the right answer versus when it's time to write code.

#7258. Brandweek 2026: Consumer-First Still Beats AI-First ft. Laura Knebusch

AI can raise the ceiling on marketing, or it can expose every weak foundation underneath it.

Live from the Marketing Vanguard stage at Brandweek 2026, Jenny Rooney sits down with Laura Knebusch, senior vice president of CPG marketing and customer experience at Georgia-Pacific, to explore what modern brand building really requires when media, data, retail, and AI are all evolving at once.

Laura gets into why Georgia-Pacific spent 18 months consolidating data sources, and how that foundation now supports in-house capabilities and agentic AI.

She also discusses why consumer-first and AI-first marketing are not actually competing ideas.

This episode is part of a special vidcast series that was recorded live during Brandweek 2026 and presented in partnership with Tatari.

Laura Knebusch is Senior Vice President of CPG Marketing and Customer Experience at Georgia-Pacific, where her team supports consumer and B2B brands across insights, content, media, promotions, marketing technology and analytics.

She began her marketing career at Procter & Gamble after earning her MBA from Duke and later joined Georgia-Pacific, where she has spent 18 years across brand management, category leadership and marketing capability roles.

Today, her focus is on combining classic brand-building fundamentals with the data, technology and operating models needed for modern CPG marketing.

Private Label Is Waiting, So What About Your Brand? — Laura points out that consumers have been adjusting their behavior for years, not reacting to one sudden economic moment. In household essentials, private-label options are credible and easy to access. There’s a warning lying there for marketers: if the brand does not create a real reason to choose it, loyalty becomes much harder to defend.

100 Data Sources, One Source of Truth, Eighteen Months of Work — When Laura stepped into her current role, Georgia-Pacific needed to become more digitally capable. The first step was not another tool. It was fixing the data. Over 18 months, the team connected roughly 100 different sources, cleaned and harmonized them, and built one accessible system that now powers multiple in-house capabilities.

Consumer-First vs. AI-First Is the Wrong Debate — At Brandweek 2026, Laura describes an internal discussion that paired behavioral science with generative-engine optimization. At first, the advice sounded different – Humans need simplicity, distinctiveness and repetition, while AI needs clear, consistent information. But the conclusion was the same: strong fundamentals work for both.

Why Georgia-Pacific Merged Shopper Marketing and National Media — Retail media has changed how consumers discover and buy, so Georgia-Pacific changed how it organizes around that reality. Laura explains why shopper marketing and national media were brought closer together, helping the team better understand where partners create strategic value and where investment should stay tactical.

Jenny Rooney is Chief Brand and Community Officer, leading strategy for the overall ADWEEK brand as well as the ways in which we serve and support our audiences with high-value content, products, partnerships and experiences, notably through our community programs such as Marketing Vanguard.

Select ADWEEK as one of your favorite sources and stay up to date on our latest content.

Mr. Peanut returns, Fuzzyeti makes his debut, Dog Chow salutes service dogs, and DoorDash makes a dash

How scarcity, storytelling, community, human taste, and local experience are reshaping high-end marketing.

Working at Samsara means you’ll help define the future of physical operations and be on a team that’s shaping an exciting array of product solutions, including Video-Based Safety, Vehicle Telematics, Apps and Driver Workflows, and Equipment Monitoring. As part of a recently public company, you’ll have the autonomy and support to make an impact as we build for the long term.

As a Sales Engineer (SE) at Samsara, you’d be part of a diverse group of technically-minded folks working together to bring our unique connected operations technology to customers. Our daily customer engagements include conversations around logistics/supply chain management, fleet maintenance strategies, global asset management, machine control systems/diagnostics, system integration, and regulatory compliance. This means a successful SE at Samsara will develop a thorough understanding of IoT hardware and sensors, hands-on installation/deployment scenarios, carrier networks, cloud infrastructure, and third-party system integrations (via our open API) to ensure the proper technical solution is presented to our customers.

This is a remote position open to candidates residing in the US within the TOLA region (Texas, Oklahoma, Louisiana, Arkansas) within the US except Alaska, California (non-Bay Area), Connecticut, Maryland, Massachusetts, New Jersey, New York, Rhode Island, Denver Metro (CO), Boulder Metro (CO),Chicago Metro (IL), Austin Metro (TX), Houston Metro (TX), Dallas Metro (TX), Seattle Metro (WA), SF Bay Metros (CA), NYC Metro (NY) and Washington DC Metro. Relocation expenses will not be provided for this role.

The compensation range shown spans approved pay zones for this role. Your primary work location determines the range applicable to you, which may be narrower than the full range displayed.

The range of annual on-target earnings (OTE) range for full-time employees for this position is below. Please note that OTE pay may vary depending on factors including your city of residence, job-related knowledge, skills, and experience. Learn more about our total rewards and benefits below.

At Samsara, we build for the people who keep the global economy moving. We want owners, not passengers, which is why our rewards are designed to fuel high-impact builders. Our compensation program delivers above-market total compensation through a combination of base salary, performance-based bonus/variable pay, and equity (for eligible roles) in a high-growth public company. We meaningfully differentiate pay for our top performers, who have the opportunity to earn above-market compensation that can outpace the broader market over time.

Beyond compensation, we provide the foundations that enable long-term success: a flexible, employee-led remote model, a professional development stipend, comprehensive health and parental leave plans, and more. If you’re ready to build for the long term and own the outcome, your journey starts here.

At Samsara, we embrace a flexible working model that caters to the diverse needs of our teams. Our offices are open for those who prefer to work in-person and we also support remote work where it aligns with our operational requirements. For certain positions, being close to one of our offices or within a specific geographic area is important to facilitate collaboration, access to resources, or alignment with our service regions. In these cases, the job description will clearly indicate any working location requirements. Our goal is to ensure that all members of our team can contribute effectively, whether they are working on-site, in a hybrid model, or fully remotely. At Samsara, we welcome everyone regardless of their background. #7251. Week of Nov 16 Evening News Ratings: NBC Nightly News Is Only Newscast with Gains

NBC Nightly News with Tom Llamas finished the week of November 16 as the only broadcast with growth in both total viewers and the Adults 25-54 demo.

Nightly News’ performance was even more impressive in the prized demo, narrowing the viewership gap with ABC World News Tonight with David Muir to just 29,000 viewers.

Note: The weekly averages of ABC World News Tonight, CBS Evening News, and NBC Nightly News are based on four days (Monday-Thursday). Friday’s (11/20/2026) ABC News’ broadcast was retitled to WNT-ABC, CBS Evening News and NBC Nightly News were retitled to CBS Evening Nws, and NBC Nitely News, respectively. These broadcasts are excluded from the weekly and season averages.

According to Nielsen national live+same-day big data and program ratings, ABC World News Tonight averaged 9.09 million total viewers and 1.267 million Adults 25-54 demo viewers for the week of Nov. 16. The evening newscast was down 2% in total viewers and 7% in the A25-54 viewers compared to the previous week. Compared to the same week in 2025 (the week beginning Nov. 17), WNT was up 14% in total viewers and 27% in the demo.

NBC Nightly News took the No. 2 spot in total viewers and the demo, averaging 7.366 million and 1.238 million viewers, respectively. Compared to the previous week, Nightly News grew by 3% in total viewers and 7% in the demo. Looking at its performance to the same week a year ago, Nightly News was up by 14% in total viewers and 43% in the demo.

CBS Evening News closed out the week of Nov. 16 with 4.628 million total viewers and 650,000 demo viewers. CBS News’ newscast was flat in total viewers and down 6% in the demo compared to the previous week. Year-ago week numbers showed CBS Evening News up 12% in total viewers and 23% in the demo.

November 16, 2026 (Nielsen Big Data + Panel):

Source: Nielsen Big Data + Panel, L+SD, Week of 11/16/2026. Season to date: Most Current Data Stream for 2026-2027 Season (9/21 – 11/22/26) and Live+7 for 2025-2026 Season (9/22 – 11/23/26). Average audience based on regularly titled telecasts only.

#7245. Want to Sell an Agency? Launch a Creator Marketing Firm

This story was originally published in On Background with Mark Stenberg, a free weekly newsletter that explores the key themes shaping the media industry. You can sign up for it here.

In recent months, the market for creator and social agencies has been, to put it lightly, brisk.

In September, the Madrid-based social agency Samy acquired Get Engaged, a U.S. social and influencer shop, in a deal valued in the low nine figures.

In October, Billion Dollar Boy Group bought Creator Authority, a business-to-business influencer agency, and the private equity firm Shamrock Capital, which owns ADWEEK, made an eight-figure investment in the social-first agency Saylor.

Earlier this month, the social-first agency LV8 merged with its sister company Mirage Digital on the back of a “high eight figures” investment from Velocity Capital Management. And last week, the private equity firm Falfurrias Management Partners took a majority stake in Freeman & Forrest, another B2B creator agency.

The deals are part of a record year, according to James Creech, founder of the creator M&A advisory firm Quartermast. There have been 30 agency acquisitions in the creator economy so far in 2026, up from 20 in all of 2025 and above the previous high of 28 in 2022, according to Quartermast data.

Overall, there have been 124 M&A creator-adjacent deals this year, the busiest on record, and roughly half of the agency deals have come since July, per Quartermast.

Behind the surge is a crop of boutiques founded during the pandemic-era influencer boom that have matured into businesses worth buying, which are run by founders who need more resources to keep up with what clients are asking for.

A specific set of firms—social-first creative shops, podcast agencies, TikTok Shop specialists, and B2B creator plays—are responsible for the majority of this interest, while the deals themselves are increasingly financed by a new cohort of buyers, including private equity firms, strategic roll-ups, and international firms. All of it is unfolding in a frothy market for creator economy deals, which is just now beginning to consolidate in response to urgent demand across a fragmented space.

“Creator marketing works,” Creech said. “Brands and their agency partners are going to continue to invest there.”

The current rush of creator agency activity has its origins in the pandemic, according to LV8 chief executive Griffin Haddrill.

Lockdown acted as a digital accelerant, pulling budgets toward creators at a moment when television and film had dried up and out-of-home advertising had stopped working.

Because acquisitions typically take nine to 12 months to come together, the resulting deal volume spiked in 2021 and 2022, slowed over the following two years, and has since normalized, Creech said.

The same period produced a wave of new agencies, many of which were founded by executives who saw the emerging relevance of the creator ecosystem and launched startups to service it. Five years later, those boutiques have matured into businesses worth buying, according to Haddrill.

FOR Scoops, Sharp Analysis, and Insider Context

For founders, selling offers the resources to keep up with clients, according to Get Engaged co-founder Ben Hiott.

“Social is becoming the center of how brands build their businesses, and clients are asking us to do more every year,” Hiott said in October.

The wave of dealmaking that followed the pandemic was primarily influencer agencies, according to Creech, while this year, the variety of targets has expanded.

In 2026, buyers have gone after social-first creative agencies, such as Get Engaged, OK Cool, and Sway Social; podcast and audio shops like Ad Results Media and Listening Party Agency; and TikTok Shop specialists including Orca, Uptickk, and Yada.

Private equity is also getting more involved in the space, investing capital after years of financing strategic acquirers, as Bridgepoint does with Samy, according to Creech. This year, Shamrock Capital took a majority stake in Saylor, Velocity became a near-equal partner in LV8, and Falfurrias bought Freeman & Forrest, among other deals.

Serial acquirers are consolidating the field as well. Influencer agency Ykone has made four acquisitions in 2026, and creator company network Residence has made two, while Samy and Publicis remain active, according to Creech. After large platform deals for Influential and Captiv8, Publicis has turned to regional acquisitions, including the French creator agency Point d’Orgue, to enter new markets, according to Creech.

For investors, the opportunity lies in stitching these shops together, according to Haddrill.

“The consumer demand was there, but the agency space was fragmented,” Haddrill said. “Now, investors are seeing this fragmentation and working to bring them together.”

B2B creator marketing has also emerged as its own category.

Creator Authority, whose clients include Amazon, Adobe, and Mastercard, was Billion Dollar Boy Group’s first acquisition, an all-cash, multimillion-dollar deal with an earnout component, according to Business Insider.

Freeman & Forrest, founded in 2024 by Emily Freeman and Forrest Brazeal after they ran influencer programs at Microsoft and Google Cloud, helps tech companies like Amazon and Google reach technical customers through creators.

And more of the deals cross borders: Get Engaged gave Samy a U.S. base after the agency built its business in Europe and Latin America, and dealmaking between the U.S. and the U.K. has picked up in both directions, according to Creech.

As the ecosystem matures, the next wave of dealmaking may come from the middle of the market, according to a creator management executive who asked not to be named.

The largest independents, such as Whalar and Influential, have already sold to Accenture Song and Publicis, respectively, which leaves room for midsize independents, many of which will come to market over the next 12 months, the executive said.

Creator management firms, which run creators’ businesses rather than simply booking their brand deals, are likely the next category to consolidate, the executive added.

The buying, in other words, is far from over, according to Creech.

“It’s mature, it’s scaled, it’s professionalized. The work is not done,” Creech said. “We’re maybe entering middle innings.”

Hearst CMO Heads Out (SCOOP): The chief marketing officer at Hearst Magazines, Sherri Chambers, will be leaving the organization in a few weeks, according to sources. Chambers, who joined the digital media firm in December 2024, confirmed the news, while a Hearst Magazines spokesperson expressed their gratitude for Chambers in a statement. The departure is the latest in a series of executive shake-ups. In July, Hearst Magazines appointed Chris Berend as its first chief content and experience officer, and in October, it named A+E executive Juliana Stock as its new president, replacing Debi Chirichella, who will become chair of the group. The legacy media firm has worked hard to cultivate a reputation for calm and consistency in an otherwise turbulent industry, but it certainly looks as if the company is aiming to turn some kind of corner.

Bunny Boss Bolts: The editor in chief of Playboy, Phillip Picardi, has left the organization after less than a year, Puck reported earlier this week. According to a company spokesperson, the move was a personal decision, with Picardi returning to Los Angeles after Playboy set up headquarters in—where else—Miami. But Picardi only began the job officially in March, meaning his tenure at the publisher only lasted a brief eight months. Given all the fanfare surrounding his appointment, I have to imagine something went seriously awry. Or Picardi, who did not return my request for comment, landed an otherwise irrefusable job elsewhere. This is disappointing because I liked the direction Playboy was heading under Picardi, reporting on the state of sex, dating, and relationships in this fraught moment—but maybe the loyal fanbase did not.

BuzzFeed Taps Influential (EXCLUSIVE): On Monday, BuzzFeed named its first chief revenue officer under its new ownership, tapping Chris Poydenis, co-founder of the creator marketing firm Influential, to lead its revenue division. The appointment comes as BuzzFeed attempts an ambitious shift in direction, positioning itself as a source of free, ad-supported television, bundled with the rest of the Allen Media Group portfolio. The decision to name a creator marketing executive as the chief sales lead clearly telegraphs the manner in which BuzzFeed would like to evolve, shifting focus to its creators and video franchise talent. Will it work? The odds are long.

Sara Kehaulani Goo is president of The Washington Post’s creator network, a new division that sits outside of the newsroom and makes sponsored short-form video series with independent creators. Goo, formerly the editor in chief of Axios, launched the first show in April, and the network has since released six series in categories ranging from personal finance to career advice.

Publishers increasingly want to work with creators, but the harder question is why creators would want to work with publishers. In our conversation, Goo and I discussed how The Post’s model works, why its creator content goes through Post fact-checking, and what a media company can offer talent who could go it alone.

Mark Stenberg: You specifically left Axios to head up this new creator division. Can you tell me about the role and how The Post is working with creators?

Sara Kehaulani Goo: I spent 12 years of my career at The Post as a reporter and editor, so it’s been nice to return in a very different role. The theory is simple: Look at where people spend their time, where they get information, where trust is growing, and where revenue is growing across media. The answer to all of those questions is creators. So, we are working with knowledge creators, people who have built big audiences around their expertise, and treating them as equal editorial and business partners.

Sara: We mostly work with creators who’ve built audiences with short-form video in lifestyle topics like personal finance, health and wellness, and career advice. Then, we develop the shows together and publish them together. Last week, we launched Her Next Move with an executive recruiter based in New York. She posts a two-minute video to her audience of a million people as a collab with us, so it reaches both audiences. We amplify it on The Post’s social accounts and publish it on our website and app. That show is sponsored by Uber.

Mark: Why would a creator want to work with the Post? Do they share in the revenue?

Sara: I won’t get into the details, but we pay them for their time. It’s similar to a contributor model, though not exactly like freelancing, because they’re entrepreneurs. A lot of them want to focus on the content, which is what journalists want too. We help build the business around them, and we provide distribution and editorial support when they need it.

Mark: Does their content go through Post fact-checking?

Sara: Yes, through my team. The standards are the same. There’s a lack of consistency in the creator world when it comes to disclosing sponsorships, and to quality in general, and we can bring that. Trust in creators is growing, but people still wonder whether they should trust what they’re watching on Instagram and who paid for it. We can help answer that.

Mark: It’s early, but how big is this now, and where is it headed?

Sara: This year has been a proof of concept. We’ve done six shows with six creators, across health and wellness, personal finance, travel, career advice, and entertainment. Now we’re deciding which ones to renew, and next year we want to do more always-on content. Brands are very interested, because every brand is thinking about its creator strategy, and finding the right creator takes a lot of effort. We take that headache off their shoulders.

Mark: Creators often relish operating on their own. How do you convince them to work with you?

Sara: Having led newsrooms, I’ve always seen my job as spotting talent, developing it, and creating an environment where people can do their best work. This is the same. Creators are entrepreneurs, and the platforms give them plenty of ways to make money on their own. But being an entrepreneur isn’t for everybody, at least not at every level. Media companies have always worked with outside experts. The structure just looks different now.

#7243. Director of Technology Partnerships at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re looking for a Director of Technology Partnerships to lead the teams responsible for growing Supabase through technology integrations and our marketplace ecosystem.

You’ll bring together two complementary motions: partnerships that embed Supabase into the products developers already use, and a marketplace that helps developers discover and connect complementary technologies to their Supabase projects. Your job is to turn both into durable sources of revenue, adoption, and customer value.

Reporting to the Head of Partnerships, you’ll lead and develop the Integrations and Marketplace teams, set their commercial strategy, and work hands-on with our most important partners. A central part of the role is working closely with AI labs to build strategic relationships, develop integrations, and bring joint opportunities to market. You’ll partner closely with Product, Engineering, Sales, Marketing, and Finance to take integrations from idea to launch—and grow their impact long after launch.

This role is based in the San Francisco Bay Area, where you’ll build relationships with leading AI, developer tools, and infrastructure companies. Supabase is remote-first, and you’ll work across a globally distributed team.

Lead, coach, and develop the Integrations and Marketplace partnerships teams, including team leads and partner managers.

Set a clear strategy for where Supabase invests across strategic technology partnerships, marketplace categories, and emerging ecosystem opportunities.

Define team objectives, partner coverage, investment priorities, and operating cadence. Make thoughtful tradeoffs between high-touch strategic partnerships and scalable programs.

Build a culture of ownership, commercial discipline, and strong cross-functional execution.

Drive revenue from technology integrations through partner-driven customer acquisition, product consumption, expansion, retention, co-sell opportunities, and commercial arrangements.

Own the commercial performance of the technology partnerships and marketplace portfolio, including forecasting and reporting on progress, risks, and return on investment.

Build and deepen relationships with AI labs, working closely with their product, engineering, business development, and executive teams to identify and execute high-impact partnership opportunities.

Develop executive relationships with strategic partners across developer tools, cloud, and adjacent infrastructure.

Lead or support negotiations for complex partnerships, including integration, referral, revenue-sharing, and joint go-to-market agreements.

Help partner managers build joint business plans that connect partner priorities to measurable outcomes for Supabase and its customers.

Lead and develop the Marketplace team, setting its commercial and partner strategy and holding the team accountable for outcomes.

Guide the team in identifying the capabilities developers need, recruiting the right partners, and helping those partners launch and grow.

Set priorities and allocate resources across strategic, high-touch partners and scalable paths for emerging and self-serve partners.

Ensure the team works effectively with Product and Engineering on integration quality, partner onboarding, discovery, activation, and the developer experience across product surfaces.

Enable the team to work with Marketing and Sales to launch integrations, enable teams, and drive ongoing adoption—not just announcements.

Establish clear measures for partner-sourced and influenced pipeline, integration adoption, revenue, consumption, retention, and marketplace performance.

Work with Finance, Data, and Partner Operations to improve attribution, reporting, forecasting, and partner revenue processes.

Represent the voice of partners and customers in product and go-to-market planning, while aligning stakeholders around shared priorities.

Identify risks early, remove blockers, and communicate decisions and outcomes clearly to leadership.

Have significant experience in technology partnerships, business development, or ecosystem leadership at a developer tools, cloud, infrastructure, or SaaS company.

Have led and developed partner-facing teams, including managers or senior individual contributors, and can balance strategic direction with hands-on execution.

Have a track record of building partnerships that produced measurable revenue, product adoption, or customer growth.

Have negotiated complex commercial agreements and understand partnership economics, including revenue share, referrals, and consumption-based models.

Have worked closely with Product and Engineering to ship and grow technical integrations. You can engage credibly with technical teams, including those at AI labs, even if you aren’t the person writing the code.

Know how to build relationships with executives while earning the trust of product, engineering, and go-to-market teams.

Use data to prioritize opportunities, assess partner performance, and make decisions when attribution is imperfect.

Are comfortable building in an evolving environment, communicating in writing, and working asynchronously across time zones.

Built or scaled a developer marketplace, integration ecosystem, or partner platform.

Worked with AI LLMs, AI app builders, databases, open-source products, or developer infrastructure.

A background in software development, with an understanding of how developers build and integrate products.

Experience growing both a small number of strategic partnerships and a broader, scalable partner program.

A strong network in the San Francisco Bay Area technology ecosystem.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

#7240. Technical Account Manager (APJ) at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re seeking a Technical Account Manager (TAM) to be the primary technical partner for our Team & customers after the sale, helping them adopt, operate, and get long-term value from Supabase.

At Supabase, TAMs are the technical owners of their assigned accounts, working closely with our most valuable customers to:

Understand each customer’s architecture, use cases, constraints, and goals.

Validate deployments and guide customers through onboarding and early success.

Proactively surface risks and optimization opportunities before they become incidents.

Coordinate internal teams on complex escalations and drive root cause analysis.

Align Supabase capabilities with the customer’s roadmap.

Successful TAMs are proactive, technically credible, and customer-first. TAM is a relationship, not a response channel: you’ll act as an extension of your customers’ teams and advocate on their behalf within Supabase.

You’ll be joining our Success team, where you will be expected to lead and innovate around the following areas of responsibility:

Act as the technical owner for your assigned accounts, documenting environments, mapping dependencies, and tracking technical risks.

Validate architecture and deployment plans after the sale, guide initial configuration and best practices, and make sure customers understand platform capabilities and limitations.

Help customers reach early success milestones to shorten time to value and reduce early friction.

Regularly review customer environments and usage patterns to identify scalability, performance, reliability, security, and compliance risks and opportunities.

Plan upgrades and lifecycle events, and run architecture reviews for upcoming initiatives, including capacity and growth planning, feature readiness, and early identification of technical blockers.

Coordinate across Support and Engineering on complex or high-impact issues. TAMs are not frontline support, but provide the context, prioritization, and follow-through that drive resolution, root cause analysis, and preventative action.

Serve as the voice of the customer to Engineering and Product, sharing specific, reproducible feedback on recurring pain points and taking part in beta and early access programs.

Partner with Sales and Customer Success on account health and retention, with technical input on renewals, expansions, and evaluations, and early flags on risks to satisfaction.

6+ years of relevant work experience in engineering, SRE, technical consulting, or similar, including operating production systems under real constraints.

Strong technical depth across database internals, scalability, security posture, and infrastructure tradeoffs. You understand a customer’s architecture as well as they do, without escalating every question.

A proactive mindset: you spot risks and optimization opportunities before the customer does, and you treat your accounts like a personal portfolio.

Sound escalation judgment: you know when to escalate, when to resolve internally, and how to communicate clearly under pressure without creating noise.

Very strong communication skills. TAMs explain complex issues to non-technical executives and root causes to senior engineers, often in the same day.

Awareness of how TAM performance shows up in retention and NRR, including surfacing expansion signals and renewal risks early.

Experience working in async or globally distributed teams.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Our Deliverability team: a Deliverability Lead who supports our highest-value accounts and leads deliverability education, and a Deliverability Specialist who onboards and ramps up large senders moving to Kit

Our infrastructure engineers, who run Kit's sending infrastructure

Our compliance and anti-abuse engineers, who build the automated checks that keep spammers off Kit

Product Specialists and the Support team, who handle the front line and escalate deliverability issues to you

Customer Success Managers (we call them Creator Growth Managers at Kit), Sales and Migrations, and Product Managers

First Month: Shadow the Deliverability team and Product Specialists on deliverability tickets, account approvals, and abuse reports, then start answering queue tickets with review. Join the daily monitoring rotation (blocklist delisting, Postmaster and delivery monitoring) and the weekly Deliverability team meeting. Learn how account approvals, compliance flags, and our in-house sending platform work. By the end of the month, work deliverability tickets and abuse reports on your own and send your first delisting requests. Project: build a recurring report showing the trending deliverability ticket reasons so we understand what’s causing how to reduce friction for customers and how to solve it

Own compliance end to end, from new-account approvals and compliance flags to pausing or closing accounts that put our shared reputation at risk.Run Kit's abuse desk. You'll write and share a strategy that covers coverage, response-time targets, escalation paths, and quality checks, then put it in place so routine spam, phishing, and blocklist reports follow clear SOPs while you take the hardest cases yourself. Be the go-to contact for day-to-day mailbox provider issues with Microsoft, Gmail, Yahoo, Comcast, and regional providers, and write an incident runbook for blocklistings and provider throttling. Track the queue with a simple scorecard (volume, first response time, and escalation rate) and share what you learn with the team. Run training sessions for Support and ship your first process or AI improvements, like cleaner ticket categorization or better account data for our AI support agent, so more deliverability tickets get solved without escalation..

First Year: Deliverability support is faster and more consistent for every creator on Kit, including the ones who never talk to a CGM. Your playbooks, help center articles, and AI-assisted workflows are what Support, CGMs, and contractors reach for first, and fewer tickets need to come to you. You've led at least one cross-team fix with infrastructure for a recurring problem you spotted in the queue, such as an alert when a creator's domain authentication breaks or a check for sudden signup spikes. You're helping shape the internal tooling and alerting our in-house sending needs, and you take part in industry groups like M3AAWG.

Deep working knowledge of email authentication (SPF, DKIM, DMARC, alignment, and BIMI basics), plus the ability to read raw message headers and SMTP bounce and deferral codes

Hands-on use of Google Postmaster Tools and Microsoft SNDS/JMRP, and comfort working with major blocklists (Spamhaus, Abusix, Barracuda, Spamcop, and others) and their delisting processes

Data fluency: you can pull bounce, complaint, and engagement data together, find the root cause, and explain it so others can act on it

Hands-on abuse handling: you can work an abuse report or escalation start to finish, from finding the account and assessing the evidence to acting, replying to the reporter, and documenting the decision

Process building: you can turn repeat work into SOPs, playbooks, and quality checks that a contractor or teammate can follow

Clear, calm written communication with creators, teammates, and postmasters

Using AI tools to research, summarize, and draft, and helping teammates do the same, with the judgment to keep a person on final compliance calls

Experience with MTA logs or delivery observability tools (KumoMTA, PowerMTA, Postfix, Kibana/Elasticsearch, or SQL and BI tools like Mode)

Working directly with mailbox provider postmaster teams

Creating educational content, like help docs, training, workshops, or podcasts

Working with creators, digital entrepreneurs, or small businesses

You pick up problems that fall between teams without waiting for permission, and you follow them through until they're closed.

#7218. Senior Analytics Engineer at Kit

Kit is the email marketing platform experts choose to grow their audience, deepen their relationships, and build a business around the work they love. For coaches, YouTubers, authors, podcasters, and other creatives, there isn't a better marketing hub to rely on to grow audiences, automate email marketing, and sell digital products — all within one platform.More importantly, there isn't a team more committed to helping creators earn a living. We're on a mission to help creators earn $1 billion using our creator marketing platform. We have always been 100% independent and 100% remote.We are proud to have built a product that our customers love, and we look for people who have enthusiasm and belief in our mission, vision, and values to join our team. We're also embracing AI thoughtfully — both in how we build and how we hire to ensure our team is adaptable, innovative, and ready for what's next.

Our Sales team needs a dedicated data partner, and creator commerce (how creators monetize by selling digital products) is becoming a bigger part of how Kit and our creators scale. Kit is growing, so we're looking for a Senior Analytics Engineer to own Kit's sales and commerce data from end to end.

Accurate sales data gives our team a clear picture of the pipeline and the confidence to make informed decisions. That’s what makes this role so important.

Your primary focus will be Sales: the dbt models that turn our CRM data into trusted pipeline, deal, and funnel metrics, and the Omni dashboards our sales leader and Senior Revenue Operations Manager use to run the business. You'll also model creator commerce data, so we understand what creators sell and earn through Kit, and you'll pick up reporting work with our Product team, such as feature usage reporting. On a small data team, we step outside our usual lanes when the need is there, so the mix will shift over time.

You'll build for two kinds of consumers: people and AI. Teammates increasingly answer their own questions by querying our semantic layer through Omni's AI and MCP connections to AI tools. That means your models, metric definitions, and documentation need to be clear and correct enough for an AI agent to use them without a data teammate in the loop.

This is a full-time individual contributor role for someone who likes being close to the business, enjoys turning messy operational data into numbers people trust, and is happy to work on both the models and the dashboards that sit on top of them. When Sales and Product have clear, reliable data, we help more creators build more valuable businesses with Kit.

You'll report to Sam Umachi, Head of Data (Linkedin).

Sales and RevOps are your primary partners, who will rely on the models and reporting you own. You'll also partner with our Product team on reporting for features like Visual Automations and on how creators use Kit.

Within our data team, you'll work alongside a Staff Analytics Engineer who owns org-wide semantic layer governance and modeling standards, a Senior Data Engineer who owns ingestion and pipelines, and a Senior Business Analytics Partner focused on revenue opportunities. This role has no direct reports.

First Week: Work through our onboarding curriculum in Notion and meet the data team, our Senior Revenue Operations Manager, the sales team, and your product partners. Get set up in our core tools: dbt Cloud, GitHub, Amazon Redshift, Omni, HubSpot, Linear, Hightouch and Slack. Set up your AI-assisted development environment and learn the shared AI workflows our data team uses. Open and merge your first small pull request so you learn our review and deployment flow early.

First Month: Map how HubSpot objects (deals, pipelines and stages, companies, contacts, and owners) flow into our dbt models, and document the gaps. Take over day-to-day ownership of the sales models and Omni dashboards that teammates have been covering. Audit current sales reporting and list where definitions differ or numbers don't reconcile. Get familiar with how commerce data (products creators sell and the revenue they earn through Kit) lands in the warehouse today. Agree with RevOps and Sales on the most important questions they need answered. Review teammates' pull requests and contribute to our shared SQL, dbt, and documentation standards. Share a written reflection with the team on what you've learned and where you'd focus first.

First Six Months: Ship and maintain well-tested, documented dbt models for core sales metrics, such as pipeline, deal velocity, stage conversion, win rates, and the sales-led funnel. Expose those metrics through the Omni semantic layer, working within the team's governance standards. Rebuild or refresh the core sales dashboards so pipeline reviews and forecasting conversations run on numbers that don't need manual reconciliation. Resolve competing sales metric definitions by writing down the options and tradeoffs, aligning with Sales and RevOps, and documenting the outcome. Model creator-level commerce data, such as products sold, revenue earned through Kit, and conversion, in partnership with data engineering. Write semantic layer descriptions, metric definitions, and documentation that let Omni's AI and MCP-connected AI tools answer routine sales questions correctly, and test them against real questions from Sales, RevOps and Product. Get Sales to the point where routine questions are answered through self-serve, whether in Omni dashboards, Omni's AI, or AI tools connected over MCP, rather than one-off requests.

First Year: Be the recognized owner of Kit's sales data, the person Sales and RevOps go to first and trust without second-guessing. Give our revenue analysis work the sales and commerce models it depends on, so deeper questions about pipeline health and creator earnings can be answered quickly, thereby enabling us to make creators more successful. Leave your domains well documented and well tested, legible to new teammates, stakeholders, and the AI agents that query our semantic layer. Help raise the team's modeling quality through code review, pairing, and sharing what you learn.

Deep dbt experience. You build modular, tested, documented dbt projects, and you're comfortable with incremental models, snapshots, macros, CI, and scheduled jobs in dbt Cloud or similar.

Advanced SQL. You reason carefully about grain, joins, and window functions, and you write queries that are readable and performant on a cloud warehouse.

Data modeling for sales and GTM. You can model deals, pipeline stages and their history, ownership changes, and funnel conversion into clean, reusable structures. You are opinionated on Kimball standards and when a concept should be a fact or dimension.

CRM data fluency. You know how sales data behaves in a CRM, including stage changes, backdated edits, and custom properties. HubSpot is preferred, but other CRM experience (e.g. Salesforce) applies just as well. We care most that you recognize common CRM patterns.

AI-assisted development. You use AI code editors and agents (such as Cursor, Claude Code, Codex, or similar) every day, and you validate their output with tests, query results, and review before anything ships.

Clear written communication. You explain metric definitions, tradeoffs, and data caveats in plain language that Sales, RevOps, and Product partners understand.

BI and semantic layer experience. You can build dashboards and semantic models in Omni, Looker, or a similar BI tool, and you design metric definitions that people and AI tools can both query correctly.

Git-based workflows. You keep pull requests small and well described, and you give thoughtful code review.

Nice to have: Redshift performance tuning (sort and distribution keys), Python for scripting or API pulls, reverse ETL, and experience with product event, billing, payments, or ecommerce data.

Around 7 or more years in analytics engineering or data engineering where you personally shipped production data models.

Several years of hands-on work building and maintaining production dbt projects.

Working directly with sales teams and RevOps, and owning reporting they relied on to run the business.

Owning a data domain over time and evolving its models as the business and its questions changed.

Working at a SaaS company, ideally one with both a self-serve and a sales-led motion. Commerce, payments, or creator economy experience is a plus, not a requirement.

Working on a small or remote team where you were trusted to make decisions independently.

Ownership and follow-through. You treat your data domains as yours. You notice problems before stakeholders do, close the loop on requests, and finish what you start.

Judgment. You match rigor to the decision at hand, know when a quick answer is enough and when a request should become a durable model, and say so clearly.

Flexibility. You're glad to step outside your primary area when another team needs help, and you balance that work against your core commitments openly.

Teach everything you know. You document as you go, pair generously, and help stakeholders understand the data well enough to use it on their own.

Work in public. You share progress, open questions, and decisions in writing where the team can see them, including what didn't work.

Creator success is our success. You remember who's on the other end. A wrong number in a pipeline review means Sales spends time on the wrong creators, and that shapes how carefully you work.

Do it with excellence. You test your work, review it carefully, and don't ship a number you can't defend.

Candor with care. You're comfortable pushing back when a request won't answer the real question, and you do it in a way that builds trust.

Needing detailed specs or a highly structured environment before you can start.

Discomfort with ambiguity. Some of our sales and commerce definitions are still evolving, and you'll help settle them.

Needing several layers of approval to make modeling decisions within your domain.

Wanting to work only on dashboards, or only on pipelines, rather than across the transformation and BI layers.

Wanting a strictly fixed scope. Sales comes first, but you'll pick up Product or other work when the business needs it.

Wanting mostly greenfield work. Much of this role is improving sales models and dashboards that already exist.

Preferring to take tickets as written rather than partnering with stakeholders on what they need.

Being skeptical of AI's capabilities, or using AI without good judgment and accountability.

Expecting a large data team with dedicated specialists for every part of the stack.
